Siegel modular forms associated to Weil representations: SL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) & GL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) \operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\&\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}) cases
Chun-Hui Wang

Abstract
. We investigate explicit modular forms of weights 1 / 2 1/2 and 3 / 2 3/2 —classical, minus, and fermionic theta series—arising from the classical Weil representation associated to SL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) \operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}) via the 2 2 -cocycles of Rao, Kudla, Perrin, Lion–Vergne and Satake–Takase. We reorganize these forms using (tensor) induction, and subsequently extend our study to the similitude group GL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) \operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}) .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Notations and conventions

Let
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
denote the usual real numbers and complex numbers. Let
i=\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
. For an element
z=u+iv\in\mathbb{C}
with
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
u=\operatorname{Re}z
,
v=\operatorname{Im}z
,
\overline{z}=u-iv
and
|z|^{2}=u^{2}+v^{2}
. The principal argument function
\operatorname{Arg}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\to[-\pi,\pi)
assigns to each
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
its uniquely defined angle
\operatorname{Arg}z\in[-\pi,\pi)
.
Define the domains:
\mathbb{H}=\{x+yi\in\mathbb{C}\mid y>0\}
and
\mathbb{H}^{\pm}=\{x+yi\in\mathbb{C}\mid y\neq 0\}
.
Let
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})=\{\cos t+i\sin t\in\mathbb{C}\mid-\pi\leq t<\pi\}
.

Let
(W=\mathbb{R}^{2},\langle,\rangle)
be a symplectic vector space of dimension
2
over
\mathbb{R}
, endowed with the symplectic form:

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=x_{1}x_{1}^{{}^{\prime}\ast}-x_{1}^{\prime}x_{1}^{\ast},

for
v=(x_{1},x_{1}^{\ast}),v^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{{}^{\prime}\ast})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
\{e_{1}=(1,0),e_{1}^{\ast}=(0,1)\}
be a symplectic basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
X=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1}\}
,
X^{\ast}=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1}^{\ast}\}
. Let
\operatorname{H}(W)=\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}
denote the Heisenberg group, defined by

(v,t)\cdot(v^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(v+v^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+\tfrac{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle}{2}),

for
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
In
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote
P(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
,
P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in P(\mathbb{R})\mid a>0\}
,
N(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
,
N_{-}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
,
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&-\sin t\\
\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}\mid-\pi\leq t<\pi\}
. Let
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\{g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid\det g=1\textrm{ or}-1\}
. In
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
, let
P^{\pm}_{>0}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\det g\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid a>0\}
,
\operatorname{O}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&\sin t\\
\mp\sin t&\pm\cos t\end{pmatrix}\mid-\pi\leq t<\pi\}
,

\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{Z})=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\mid a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
F_{2}=\langle s(-1)\rangle
for
s(-1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}^{\pm}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\operatorname{H}^{\pm}(W)=F_{2}\ltimes(\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R})
, with the action:
(x,y;t)^{s(-1)}=(x,-y;-t)
.

Let
V=\mathbb{C}
, and let
\operatorname{H}(V)=\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{R}
denote another Heisenberg group with the multiplication:

(z,t)(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(z+z^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}(\overline{z}z^{\prime})).

Let
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})=\langle\sigma\rangle
act on
\operatorname{H}(\mathbb{C})
by
(z,t)^{\sigma}=(z^{\sigma},-t)
. Form the semi-direct product
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})\ltimes\operatorname{H}(\mathbb{C})
with this action.

Let
(\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,)_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\{\pm 1\}
denote the Hilbert symbol(extending the classical definition for use), given by

(a,b)_{\mathbb{R}}=\begin{cases}-1,&a<0\ \text{and}\ b<0,\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For any real number
a
, define

((a)):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }a\in\mathbb{Z},\\
a-\lfloor a\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For coprime integers
c,d
with
c>0
, the Dedekind sum
s(d,c)
is defined by

s(d,c)=\sum_{k=1}^{c}\Bigl(\!\Bigl(\frac{k}{c}\Bigr)\!\Bigr)\Bigl(\!\Bigl(\frac{kd}{c}\Bigr)\!\Bigr).

For elements in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
\omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
u(b)=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
,
u_{-}(c)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
c&1\end{pmatrix}
,
h(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
,
h_{\epsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\epsilon\end{pmatrix}
.

If
\psi
is a non-trivial character of
\mathbb{R}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we will write
\psi^{a}
for the character:
t\longrightarrow\psi(at)
. We will let
\psi_{0}
denote the fixed character of
\mathbb{R}
defined as:
t\longmapsto e^{2\pi it}
, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mu_{n}=\langle e^{\tfrac{2\pi i}{n}}\rangle
,
e^{\tfrac{2\pi i}{n}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
\mathbb{T}=\{e^{i\theta}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}=\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. For a finite set
G
, let
|G|
denote the cardinality of
G
. On
\mathbb{R}
, let
du
denote the Lebsegue measure. On
\mathbb{C}
, let
dz=du+idv
, and
d(z)=dudv
, unless overstated.

1.2.
Weil representations

Let
\psi
be a non-trivial continuous unitary character of
\mathbb{R}
. According to the Stone-von Neumann’s theorem, there exists only one unitary irreducible complex representation of
\operatorname{H}(W)
with central character
\psi
, known as the Heisenberg representation. Details are in Section
5
.

According to Weil’s work, the Heisenberg representation can be extended to a projective representation of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and then to an actual representation of a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-covering group over
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. This group is called the Metaplectic group, and the representation is called the Weil representation. In the real field case, this special central cover can descend to an
8
-degree or
2
-degree cover over
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. There are three common models for realizing the Weil representation: the Schödinger model, the Lattice model and the Fock model. Details are in Section
5
.

Various cocycles are linked to Weil representations. Each has unique characteristics and historical origins. In the present context,
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
is a
\mu_{8}
-valued cocycle on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, whereas
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
represents a
\mu_{2}
-valued cocycle on the same group. Their difference stems from the constant
m_{X}^{\ast}

and the relation established in Lem.
2.1
(3). Neither of these cocycles is continuous. For further details on this aspect, we refer the reader to Rao
[
25
]
, Kudla
[
15
]
, Perrin
[
22
]
, and Lion-Vergne
[
18
]
. We mention that these works also treat the case of higher-rank symplectic groups. In
[
31
]
, Takase introduced another real analytic cocycle
\widehat{c}_{z}
linked to the Fock model of the Weil representation, which is useful in studying the irreducible
\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-components of the Weil representation. We reorganize their results in Sections
2
and
5
. For our purpose, we extend the
2
-cocycles and Weil representations from
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
in Sections
3
and
6
, following Barthel’s work
[
3
]
on the
p
-adic field. For a deeper analysis of the Gobal field case, we direct the reader to Waldspurger’s seminal works
[
35
,
36
]
.

Throughout this paper, lowercase symbols (
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\widehat{c}_{z}
) indicate
2
-cocycles on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, while uppercase symbols (
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\widehat{C}_{z}
) indicate their counterparts on
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The associated central extension groups are denoted by
\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}^{z}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\overline{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}^{z}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively. When
z=z_{0}
, we abbreviate
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}^{z_{0}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}^{z_{0}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively. The notations for the corresponding central covering subgroups follow the same convention.

Lowercase
\pi
always denotes the Weil representation of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, while uppercase
\Pi
denotes that of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.

1.3.
Modular forms of half-integral weight

Theta functions, as is well known, have a long and rich history. In what follows, we mention only the papers and results most relevant to our work. For broader historical context and recent developments, we refer the reader to the introduction in
[
30
]
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{H}
. According to D. Zagier’s book
[
39
, p.27]
, the theta series, the minus theta series, and the fermionic theta series are defined respectively by:

\theta(\tfrac{z}{2})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\pi zn^{2}},\quad\theta^{M}(\tfrac{z}{2})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}e^{i\pi zn^{2}},\quad\theta^{F}(\tfrac{z}{2})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\pi z(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}}.

Moreover, these satisfy the identity(cf.
[
39
, pp. 27–30]
)

\theta^{M}(\tfrac{z}{2})\cdot\theta(\tfrac{z}{2})\cdot\theta^{F}(\tfrac{z}{2})=2\eta^{3}(z),

where
\eta(z)
is the Dedekind eta-function. By Shimura’s classical paper
[
29
]
on modular forms of half-integral weight, the theta series
\theta(z)
is a modular form of weight
1/2
for the group
\Gamma_{0}(4)
satisfying the equality:

Theorem 1.1
.

\theta(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\nu(r)\sqrt{cz+d}\theta(z)
, for
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{0}(4),\nu(r)=\left(\frac{c}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}
.

In Lion-Vergne’s book
[
18
]
, the authors approach theta series via Weil representations. Indeed, in
[
18
, Sect. 2.4.13, Theorem]
, they state the result for
\theta(\tfrac{z}{2})
rather than
\theta(z)
. According to the theory of modular forms(cf.
[
11
]
), these two functions differ only by a certain slash operator. Let:

(1)

\theta^{1/2}(z)=\theta(\tfrac{z}{2})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\pi zn^{2}}
;

(2)

\Gamma_{\theta}=\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\mid ac\equiv 0(\bmod 2),bd\equiv 0(\bmod 2)\}
;

(3)

\lambda(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\left(\frac{2c}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d},&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2),\\
\left(\frac{2d}{c}\right)\epsilon_{c}e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}(c,d)_{\mathbb{R}},&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{\theta}\setminus\Gamma(2).\end{array}\right.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
18
, Sect. 2.4.13, Theorem]
)
.

\theta^{1/2}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\lambda(r)\sqrt{cz+d}\,\theta^{1/2}(z)
, for
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{\theta}
,
z\in\mathbb{H}
.

Note that for
r\in\Gamma(2)
, the above result is comparable with Shimura’s Theorem
1.1
. The function
\lambda(r)
above is adopted from
[
7
, p.42, Prop.2.3.3.]
, which coincides with the expression given by Lion-Vergne in
[
18
, Sect.2.4.9, Thm.]
.

Let
N
be a positive integer and
\chi
is a primitive Dirichlet character modulo
N
. We say
\chi
even if
\chi(-1)=1
and odd if
\chi(-1)=-1
. When
N=1
, we have
\chi\equiv 1
. Let
t
be a positive integer.

Definition 1.3
.

(1)

If
\chi
is even, define
\theta^{+}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n)\,e^{2\pi itzn^{2}}
.

(2)

If
\chi
is odd, define
\theta^{-}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n)\,ne^{2\pi itzn^{2}}
.

Theorem 1.4

(
[
18
, Sect. 2.4.19, Theorem]
)
.

Let
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{0}(4N^{2}t)
,
z\in\mathbb{H}
. Then:

(1)

\theta^{+}_{\chi,t}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\chi(d)\left(\frac{t}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}\left(\frac{c}{d}\right)(cz+d)^{1/2}\,\theta^{+}_{\chi,t}(z)
.

(2)

\theta^{-}_{\chi,t}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\chi(d)\left(\frac{t}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}\left(\frac{c}{d}\right)(cz+d)^{3/2}\,\theta^{-}_{\chi,t}(z)
.

Using the same framework, and aided by the works of Takase–Satake on Fock models for Weil representations (see
[
26
,
27
,
31
,
32
]
), we extend the above results to the minus theta series and the fermionic theta series. The corresponding multiplier systems are computed explicitly. Let
\kappa=1
or
3
,
\mathfrak{w}=\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Definition 1.5
.

•

If
\chi
is even, define:

(1)

\theta^{1/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n)\,e^{i\pi tzn^{2}}
,

(2)

\theta^{M,1/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}\chi(n)\,e^{i\pi tzn^{2}}
,

(3)

\theta^{F,1/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n+\tfrac{N+1}{2})e^{i\pi tz(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}}
, for odd
N
.

•

If
\chi
is odd, define:

(1)

\theta^{3/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n)\,ne^{i\pi tzn^{2}}
,

(2)

\theta^{M,3/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}\chi(n)\,ne^{i\pi tzn^{2}}
,

(3)

\theta^{F,3/2}_{\chi,t}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi(n+\tfrac{N+1}{2})(n+\tfrac{1}{2})e^{i\pi tz(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}}
, for odd
N
.

Definition 1.6
.

(1)

\nu_{\theta,t}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2),\\
\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}(-ct,d)_{\mathbb{R}},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2)\omega.\end{array}\right.

(2)

\nu_{\theta^{M},t}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}(\tfrac{-1}{d})^{\tfrac{c}{2t}}i^{\tfrac{c}{2t}},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2),\\
\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}(\tfrac{-1}{d})^{\tfrac{c}{2t}}i^{\tfrac{c}{2t}}e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2)u_{-}(-1).\end{array}\right.

(3)

\nu_{\theta^{F},t}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}(\tfrac{-1}{d})^{\tfrac{bt}{2}}i^{\tfrac{bt}{2}},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2),\\
\left(\frac{2ct}{d}\right)\epsilon^{-1}_{d}(\tfrac{-1}{d})^{\tfrac{bt}{2}}i^{\tfrac{bt}{2}}e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}},&&r=\begin{pmatrix}a&tb\\
ct^{-1}&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(2)u(1).\end{array}\right.

Proposition A
.

(1)

\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\nu_{\theta,t}(r)(cz+d)^{\kappa/2}\chi(d)\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(z)
, for
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{w}^{-1}[\Gamma_{\theta}\cap\Gamma_{0}(N^{2})]\mathfrak{w}
.

(2)

\theta^{M,\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\nu_{\theta^{M},t}(r)(cz+d)^{\kappa/2}\chi(d)\theta^{M,\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(z)
, for
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{w}^{-1}[\Gamma^{0}(2)\cap\Gamma_{0}(N^{2})]\mathfrak{w}
.

(3)

\theta^{F,\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(\tfrac{az+b}{cz+d})=\nu_{\theta^{F},t}(r)(cz+d)^{\kappa/2}\chi(d)\theta^{F,\kappa/2}_{\chi,t}(z)
, for
r=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{w}^{-1}[\Gamma_{0}(2)\cap\Gamma_{0}(N^{2})]\mathfrak{w}
.

Proof.

See Section
11
, Propositions
11.25
,
11.29
,
11.33
.
∎

The first item aligns with Lion-Vergne’s theorem (Thm.
1.4
) modulo the slash operator corresponding to
\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. To establish this result, we examine and compare three models of the Weil representation—the Schrödinger model, the lattice model, and the Fock model. These modular forms can be interpreted as intertwining operators, a perspective dating back to
[
19
]
. The underlying methodology can also be applied to the study of Siegel modular forms for higher-rank metaplectic groups, as demonstrated in
[
37
]
. To compute the explicit multiplier systems, we use the corresponding
2
-cocycles associated with
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to construct the relevant slash operators, following the approach of Lion-Vergne
[
18
]
. One can see Section
11
for the details.

1.4.
The results

In the Langlands program, the theory is formulated not only in terms of modular forms, but also in the language of automorphic representations (cf.
[
13
]
,
[
16
]
for advanced treatments). Under the same spirit, we recast the result above in the language of representation theory and generate it by making use of induced representations and tensor-induced representations. Our treatment of representations is based on the works of Bushnell-Henniart
[
5
]
, Curtis-Reiner
[
8
]
, and Serre
[
28
]
.

Let:

\overline{\lambda}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\theta}\to\mathbb{T};\quad[r,\epsilon]\longmapsto\lambda(r)\epsilon,

\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\theta}\cap\overline{\Gamma}_{0}(N^{2})\to\mathbb{T};\quad[r,\epsilon]\longmapsto\lambda(r)\chi(r)\epsilon,

\overline{\gamma}_{\chi}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\theta}\cap\overline{\Gamma}_{0}(N^{2})}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{0}(N^{2})}[\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}]^{-1}.

By Lemmas
4.7
and
9.4
, both
\overline{\lambda}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}
are characters. Moreover, by Lemmas
9.11
and
9.14
(1),
\overline{\gamma}_{\chi}
is irreducible if
N
is odd or
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
; whereas by Lemma
9.14
(2),
\overline{\gamma}_{\chi}
is reducible if
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.

•

For odd
N
, define
\overline{M}_{q_{1}}=[u(N),1]
,
\overline{M}_{q_{2}}=[1,1]
, and
\overline{M}_{q_{3}}=[u_{-}(-N),1]
; for even
N
, define
\overline{M}_{q_{1}}=[u(1),1]
and
\overline{M}_{q_{2}}=[1,1]
.

•

For odd
N
, define

\Theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}:\mathbb{H}\longrightarrow M_{1\times 3}(\mathbb{C});\quad z\longmapsto\Big([\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\overline{M}_{q_{1}}}(z),[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\overline{M}_{q_{2}}}(z),[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\overline{M}_{q_{3}}}(z)\Big).

•

For even
N
, define

\Theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}:\mathbb{H}\longrightarrow M_{1\times 2}(\mathbb{C});\quad z\longmapsto\Big([\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\overline{M}_{q_{1}}}(z),[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\overline{M}_{q_{2}}}(z)\Big).

•

V_{G\to H}
: the transfer map from
G
to
H
.

Theorem B
.

Let
g\in G=\overline{\Gamma}_{0}(N^{2})
,
h\in H=\overline{\Gamma}_{\theta}\cap\overline{\Gamma}_{0}(N^{2})
,
z\in\mathbb{H}
,
\kappa=1
or
3
.

(1)

\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}(hz)=J_{\kappa/2}(h,z)\,\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}(h)\,\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}(z)
.

(2)

\Theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}(gz)=J_{\kappa/2}(g,z)\,\Theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}(z)\,\overline{\gamma}_{\chi}(g^{-1})
.

(3)

(a)

If
N
is odd, then
[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\otimes}(gz)=J_{\kappa/2}(g,z)^{3}\,[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\otimes}(z)\,\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}(V_{G\to H}(g))
.

(b)

If
N
is even, then
[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\otimes}(gz)=J_{\kappa/2}(g,z)^{2}\,[\theta^{\kappa/2}_{\chi}]^{\otimes}(z)\,\overline{\lambda}_{\chi}(V_{G\to H}(g))
.

Proof.

For part (1), see Theorem
9.5
; for part (2), see Theorems
9.12
and
9.15
; for part (3), see Theorem
9.16
.
∎

In Section
10
, we extend the above results to the similitude group
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The main result is as follows.

Theorem C
.

Let
g\in G=\overline{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{0}(N^{2})
,
h\in H=\overline{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{\theta}\cap\overline{\Gamma}^{\pm}_{0}(N^{2})
,
z\in\mathbb{H}^{\pm}
,
\delta=\delta^{+}
or
\delta^{-}
.

(1)

\theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(hz)=J_{\delta}(h,z)\,\theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(z)\,\overline{\lambda}^{\pm}_{\chi}(h^{-1})
.

(2)

\Theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(gz)=J_{\delta}(g,z)\,\Theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(z)\,\overline{\gamma}^{\pm}_{\chi}(g^{-1})
.

Proof.

See Theorem
10.6
for part (1) and Theorem
10.11
for part (2).
∎

The notation and conventions are similar. The main difference in the similitude case is that we employ the Weil–Deligne group to define the automorphic factor
J_{\delta}(-,z)
in Section
10.2
. Additionally, both
\theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(z)
and
\Theta^{\pm,\delta}_{\chi}(z)
are matrix-valued functions. In Lemma
10.5
, we prove that
\overline{\lambda}^{\pm}_{\chi}
is irreducible. Similarly, by Lemma
10.10
,
\overline{\gamma}^{\pm}_{\chi}
is irreducible if
N
is odd or
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, and reducible if
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.

2.
The relevant covering groups:
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
case

Let
\psi=\psi_{0}^{\mathfrak{e}}
be a non-trivial character of
\mathbb{R}
, for some
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. In this section, we use the following notations:
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,
z\in\mathbb{H}
.

2.1.
The cocycles
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
and
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}

Let us define:

(1)

x(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}d\mathbb{R}^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
c\mathbb{R}^{\times 2}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

(2)

\gamma(\psi)=\psi_{0}(\tfrac{\operatorname{sgn}\mathfrak{e}}{8})=e^{\tfrac{(\operatorname{sgn}\mathfrak{e})\pi i}{4}}.

(3)

\gamma(a,\psi)=\tfrac{\gamma(\psi^{a})}{\gamma(\psi)}.

(4)

m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e^{\tfrac{i\pi(\operatorname{sgn}\mathfrak{e})}{4}[1-\operatorname{sgn}(d)]}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
e^{\tfrac{i\pi(\operatorname{sgn}\mathfrak{e})}{4}[-\operatorname{sgn}(c)]}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

If
\psi=\psi_{0}
, we write
m_{X^{\ast}}
for
m_{X^{\ast},\psi}
for simplicity.

Lemma 2.1
.

(1)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=(x(g_{1}),x(g_{2}))_{\mathbb{R}}(-x(g_{1})x(g_{2}),x(g_{3}))_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(2)

\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}})=e^{\tfrac{i\pi(\operatorname{sgn}\mathfrak{e})}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(c_{1}c_{2}c_{3})}.

(3)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})
.

Proof.

See
[
25
, p.364, Remark]
for (1),
[
25
, p.359, Coro.4.3]
for (2) and
[
25
, p.360,(5.1)]
for (3).
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
p\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)=1=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,p)
and
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,p)=1
.

Proof.

The first equalities follow from Lemma (2) above.
For the second, observe that

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)

\displaystyle=(x(p),x(g))_{\mathbb{R}}\,(-x(p)x(g),x(pg))_{\mathbb{R}}=(-x(p)x(g),x(p)x(g))_{\mathbb{R}}=1,

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,p)

\displaystyle=(x(g),x(p))_{\mathbb{R}}\,(-x(g)x(p),x(gp))_{\mathbb{R}}=(-x(g)x(p),x(g)x(p))_{\mathbb{R}}=1.

∎

2.1.1.
K=\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

Note that
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, through the mapping
(\cos t+i\sin t)\longrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&-\sin t\\
\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}
. Following
[
18
, pp.75-77]
, let us define the function
u:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
by

u(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}2k&\textrm{ if }t=k\pi,\\
2k+1&\textrm{ if }k\pi<t<(k+1)\pi.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 2.3
.

\operatorname{sgn}[\sin(t_{1})\sin(t_{2})\sin(t_{1}+t_{2})]=u(t_{1})+u(t_{2})-u(t_{1}+t_{2})
.

Proof.

See
[
18
, pp.75-77]
.
∎

Let us modify the above
u
by a character of
\mathbb{R}
in the following way:

u^{\prime}(t)=u(t)+\tfrac{2}{\pi}t.

Lemma 2.4
.

(1)

u^{\prime}(t+k\pi)=u^{\prime}(t)+4k
, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

\operatorname{sgn}[\sin(t_{1})\sin(t_{2})\sin(t_{1}+t_{2})]=u^{\prime}(t_{1})+u^{\prime}(t_{2})-u^{\prime}(t_{1}+t_{2})
.

Proof.

1) Note that
u(t+k\pi)=u(t)+2k
. So
u^{\prime}(t+k\pi)=u(t+k\pi)+\tfrac{2}{\pi}(t+k\pi)=u(t)+2k+\tfrac{2}{\pi}t+2k=u^{\prime}(t)+4k
.

2) Since
u^{\prime}
is different from
u
by a character of
\mathbb{R}
, the result follows from the above lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

The restriction of
[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
is trivial, with an explicit trivialization:

\widetilde{s}:\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\ast};\cos t+i\sin t\longmapsto e^{-\tfrac{i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})}{4}u^{\prime}(t)},

such that
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

It is well defined by the above lemma (1).

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(e^{it_{1}},e^{it_{2}})

\displaystyle=e^{\tfrac{i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})}{4}\operatorname{sgn}[\sin(t_{1})\sin(t_{2})\sin(t_{1}+t_{2})]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. \ref {chap6theq}(2)}}}{{=}}e^{\tfrac{i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})}{4}u^{\prime}(t_{1})}e^{\tfrac{i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})}{4}u^{\prime}(t_{2})}e^{\tfrac{-i\pi\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})}{4}u^{\prime}(t_{1}+t_{2})}.

∎

Lemma 2.6
.

The restriction of
[\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}]
on
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
is trivial, with an explicit trivialization:

\overline{s}(e^{it})=e^{\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{it}{2}}
, for
-\pi\leq t<\pi
,
such that
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

By (
2.1
) and the above lemma, we have:

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=[\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}][\widetilde{s}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})^{-1}]^{-1}[\widetilde{s}(g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})^{-1}]^{-1}.

u(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}0&\textrm{ if }t=0,\\
1&\textrm{ if }0<t<\pi,\\
-1&\textrm{ if }-\pi<t<0,\\
-2&\textrm{ if }t=-\pi.\end{array}\right.

\widetilde{s}(e^{it})=e^{-\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{it}{2}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}1&\textrm{ if }t=0,\\
e^{-\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }0<t<\pi,\\
e^{\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }-\pi<t<0,\\
e^{\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}&\textrm{ if }t=-\pi.\end{array}\right.m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(e^{it})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}1&\textrm{ if }t=0,\\
e^{-\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }0<t<\pi,\\
e^{\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}&\textrm{ if }-\pi<t<0,\\
e^{\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}&\textrm{ if }t=-\pi.\end{array}\right.

Hence:

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1},

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}=\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2}).

∎

Remark 2.7
.

(1)

The trivialization map for
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
can not be valued in
\mu_{8}
.

(2)

The trivialization map for
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
can not be valued in
\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

Assume, for contradiction, that a second trivialization

\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\colon\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})\to\mu_{8}
exists.
Then
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}=\widetilde{s}\cdot\chi
for some measurable homomorphism

\chi\colon\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})\to\mu_{8}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
.
Because
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
is separable,
\chi
is automatically continuous,
hence of the form
\chi(e^{it})=e^{int}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus

e^{int}\cdot e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})it}\in\mu_{8}\quad\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{R};

this is impossible.
Hence no such
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
exists.
The second statement is proved in the same way.
∎

2.2.
The cocycle
\widehat{c}_{z}

Let
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{C}
with
x>0
. Then
z=re^{it}
with
-\tfrac{\pi}{2}<t<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
and
\sqrt{z}^{-1}=\sqrt{r}^{-1/2}e^{-it/2}
. Let
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}
. Define:

•

\gamma(z^{\prime},z)=\big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\big)^{-1/2}\cdot(\operatorname{Im}z^{\prime})^{1/4}\cdot(\operatorname{Im}z)^{1/4}
.

•

\epsilon(g;z^{\prime},z)=\frac{\gamma(gz^{\prime},gz)}{\gamma(z^{\prime},z)}
.

•

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})=\epsilon(g_{1};z,g_{2}(z))
.

•

\alpha_{z}(g)=\tfrac{cz+d}{|cz+d|}
.

•

J(g,z)=cz+d
.

According to
[
31
]
,
\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})
defines a real-analytic
2
-cocycle of order
2
.

Lemma 2.8
.

\epsilon(g;z^{\prime},z)=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz^{\prime}+d)^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz+d)^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}
.

Proof.

\operatorname{Im}(gz)=\tfrac{\operatorname{Im}(z)}{|cz+d|^{2}}=\tfrac{\operatorname{Im}(z)}{|J(g,z)|^{2}}.

\displaystyle\epsilon(g;z^{\prime},z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{g(z^{\prime})-\overline{g(z)}}{2i}\Big)^{-1/2}\cdot\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot|J(g,z^{\prime})|^{-1/2}\cdot|J(g,z)|^{-1/2}

(2.1)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\tfrac{1}{(cz^{\prime}+d)(c\overline{z}+d)}\Big)^{-1/2}\cdot\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot|J(g,z^{\prime})|^{-1/2}\cdot|J(g,z)|^{-1/2}

(2.2)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\cdot\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}\Big)^{-1/2}\cdot\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\Big)^{1/2}.

(2.3)

Write
\theta=\operatorname{Arg}(z^{\prime}-\overline{z})
,
\theta_{1}=\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d)
,
\theta_{2}=\operatorname{Arg}(cz^{\prime}+d)
. Note:

cz^{\prime}+d-(c\overline{z}+d)=c(z^{\prime}-\overline{z});

\operatorname{Arg}(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i})=\theta-\tfrac{\pi}{2};

•

If
c=0
, then
\epsilon(g;z^{\prime},z)=1=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz^{\prime}+d)^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz+d)^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}
.

•

If
c>0
, then
\theta
,
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
all belong to
(0,\pi)
.

\operatorname{Arg}(c\overline{z}+d)=-\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d)\in(-\pi,0),\quad\operatorname{Arg}[-(c\overline{z}+d)]=\pi+\operatorname{Arg}(c\overline{z}+d)=\pi-\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d).

\tfrac{\operatorname{Arg}(cz^{\prime}+d)+\operatorname{Arg}[-(c\overline{z}+d)]}{2}=\operatorname{Arg}[c(z^{\prime}-\overline{z})]=\operatorname{Arg}(z^{\prime}-\overline{z}).

\tfrac{\theta_{2}+\pi-\theta_{1}}{2}=\theta,\quad\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\pi-2\theta.

\operatorname{Arg}\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\cdot\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}\Big)=\operatorname{Arg}[\exp(\theta-\tfrac{\pi}{2}+\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})]=\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\theta;

\operatorname{Arg}\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\cdot\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}\Big)=\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}-\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}+\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}.

\displaystyle(\ref {equ3})

\displaystyle=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz^{\prime}+d)^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz+d)^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}.

(2.4)

•

If
c<0
, then
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
both belong to
(-\pi,0)
.

\operatorname{Arg}(c\overline{z}+d)=-\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d)\in(0,\pi),\quad\operatorname{Arg}[-(c\overline{z}+d)]=\operatorname{Arg}(c\overline{z}+d)-\pi=-\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d)-\pi.

\tfrac{\operatorname{Arg}(cz^{\prime}+d)+\operatorname{Arg}[-(c\overline{z}+d)]}{2}=\operatorname{Arg}[c(z^{\prime}-\overline{z})]=\theta-\pi.

\tfrac{\theta_{2}-\pi-\theta_{1}}{2}=\theta-\pi,\quad\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\pi-2\theta.

\operatorname{Arg}\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\cdot\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}\Big)=\operatorname{Arg}[\exp(\theta-\tfrac{\pi}{2}+\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})]=\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\theta;

\operatorname{Arg}\Big(\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}\cdot\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}\Big)=\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{z^{\prime}-\overline{z}}{2i}-\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{(cz^{\prime}+d)}{|cz^{\prime}+d|}+\operatorname{Arg}\tfrac{(cz+d)}{|cz+d|}.

\displaystyle(\ref {equ3})

\displaystyle=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz^{\prime}+d)^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz+d)^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}.

(2.5)

∎

Lemma 2.9
.

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})^{2}=\alpha_{z}(g_{1})\alpha_{z}(g_{2})\alpha_{z}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
g_{2}(z)=z^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon(g_{1};z,g_{2}(z))^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}\Bigg\}^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{i\operatorname{Arg}(c_{1}z+d_{1})}-e^{i\operatorname{Arg}(c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{c_{1}z+d_{1}}{|c_{1}z+d_{1}|}\cdot\Big[\tfrac{c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1}}{|c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1}|}\Big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{J(g_{1},z)}{|J(g_{1},z)|}\cdot\Big[\tfrac{J(g_{1},g_{2}(z))}{|J(g_{1},g_{2}(z))|}\Big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{J(g_{1},z)}{|J(g_{1},z)|}\cdot\tfrac{J(g_{2},z)}{|J(g_{2},z)|}\cdot\Big[\tfrac{J(g_{1}g_{2},z)}{|J(g_{1}g_{2},z)|}\Big]^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
g_{1}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
and
g_{2}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. Then
\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g)=1=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g,g_{2})
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

1) Write
g_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
0&a_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
, with
a_{1}>0
. By
[
31
, p.118]
,

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{g_{1}(i)-\overline{g_{1}g(i)}}{2i}\Big)^{-1/2}\cdot\Big(\tfrac{i-\overline{g(i)}}{2i}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot|J(g_{1},i)|^{-1/2}\cdot|J(g_{1},g(i))|^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\Big(a_{1}^{2}\tfrac{i-\overline{g(i)}}{2i}\Big)^{-1/2}\cdot\Big(\tfrac{i-\overline{g(i)}}{2i}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot a_{1}

\displaystyle=1.

2)
g_{2}(i)=i
and
\gamma(i,i)=1
.

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g,g_{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma(g(i),g(i))

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{g(i)-\overline{g(i)}}{2i}\big)^{-1/2}\cdot(\operatorname{Im}g(i))^{1/4}\cdot(\operatorname{Im}g(i))^{1/4}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Im}g(i))^{-1/2}\cdot(\operatorname{Im}g(i))^{1/2}

\displaystyle=1.

∎

For any
z\in\mathbb{H}
, write
z=p_{z}(i)
, for some
p_{z}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.11
.

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{p_{z}},g_{2}^{p_{z}})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z}\big(g_{1},g_{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\big(g_{1};z,g_{2}(z)\big)=\tfrac{\gamma\big(g_{1}(z),g_{1}g_{2}(z)\big)}{\gamma\big(z,g_{2}(z)\big)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\gamma\big(g_{1}p_{z}(i),g_{1}g_{2}p_{z}(i)\big)}{\gamma\big(p_{z}(i),g_{2}p_{z}(i)\big)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\gamma\big(g_{1}p_{z}(i),g_{1}p_{z}[p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}](i)\big)}{\gamma\big(i,p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}(i)\big)}\cdot\tfrac{\gamma\big(i,p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}(i)\big)}{\gamma\big(p_{z}(i),g_{2}p_{z}(i)\big)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}\big(g_{1}p_{z},p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}\big)\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}\big(p_{z},p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}\big(g_{1}p_{z},p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}\big)

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}\big(p_{z}^{-1}g_{1}p_{z},p_{z}^{-1}g_{2}p_{z}\big).

∎

Let
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}^{\mu_{8}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}^{z}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
denote the central extensions of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
arising from
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
, and
\widehat{c}_{z}
, with centers
\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{8}
,
\mu_{8}
, and
\mathbb{T}
, respectively.

2.3.
Link
\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}
to
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
and
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}

In Lemma
2.6
, we have defined
\overline{s}
for
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. Assume
\psi=\psi_{0}
there. Let us extend this function to the group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
as follows:

g=p_{g}k_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&\tfrac{bd+ac}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\\
0&\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&-\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\\
\tfrac{c}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}&\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\end{pmatrix},

(2.6)

for
p_{g}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
k_{g}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. We define:

\displaystyle\overline{s}(g)=\overline{s}(k_{g})=e^{\tfrac{it}{2}}=\tfrac{\sqrt{ci+d}}{\sqrt[4]{c^{2}+d^{2}}},

(2.7)

for
k_{g}=e^{it}=\tfrac{ci+d}{\sqrt{c^{2}+d^{2}}}
, with
-\pi\leq t<\pi
. Note that

\overline{s}(g)^{2}=\alpha_{z_{0}}(g).

(2.8)

Lemma 2.12
.

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
p=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
u=\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&-\sin t\\
\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C}),u_{1},u_{2}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
,
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

(1)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)\overline{s}(p)\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(pg)^{-1}=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,g)=1=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(p,g)
.

(2)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{1},u_{2})\overline{s}(u_{1})\overline{s}(u_{2})\overline{s}(u_{1}u_{2})^{-1}=1=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(u_{1},u_{2})
.

(3)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(pu_{1},u_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,u_{1})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(pu_{1},u_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(p,u_{1}u_{2})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{1},u_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{1},u_{2})
;

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(pu_{1},u_{2})\overline{s}(pu_{1})\overline{s}(u_{2})\overline{s}(pu_{1}u_{2})^{-1}=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u_{1},u_{2})\overline{s}(u_{1})\overline{s}(u_{2})\overline{s}(u_{1}u_{2})^{-1}=1=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(pu_{1},u_{2}).

(4)

Write
up=p^{\prime}u^{\prime}
, for
p^{\prime}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
u^{\prime}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. Then:

(a)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(u,p)\overline{s}(u)\overline{s}(p)\overline{s}(up)^{-1}=\overline{s}(u)\overline{s}(up)^{-1}=\overline{s}(u)\overline{s}(u^{\prime})^{-1}
.

(b)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(u,p(z_{0}))\big]^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(u,p(z_{0}))J(p,z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(up,z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(p^{\prime},u^{\prime}(z_{0}))J(u^{\prime},z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[\big[J(p^{\prime},z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big[J(u^{\prime},z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\big(J(u^{\prime},z_{0})^{1/2}\big).

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(u,p)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(u;z_{0},p(z_{0}))

\displaystyle=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(u,z_{0})\big]^{1/2}\big)-\operatorname{Arg}\big(\big[J(u,p(z_{0}))\big]^{1/2}\big)\Big]}

\displaystyle=\overline{s}(u)\overline{s}(u^{\prime})^{-1}.

(5)

Write
g_{i}=p_{i}u_{i}
for
p_{i}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
u_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. Note that the right hand side also defines a cocycle and we write it by
c^{\prime}
. Then:

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(p_{1}u_{1},p_{2}u_{2})=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(u_{1},p_{2});

c^{\prime}(p,g)=1=c^{\prime}(g,u),\textrm{ for }p\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R}),u\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C}).

c^{\prime}(g_{1},g_{2})=c^{\prime}(p_{1}u_{1},p_{2}u_{2})=c^{\prime}(u_{1},p_{2})=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(u_{1},p_{2}).

∎

For
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}(g)=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)\overline{s}(g).

(2.9)

Note that in Lemma
2.5
we have already defined a function
\widetilde{s}(g)
for
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
.

(1)

If
\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})<0
, the restriction of
\widetilde{s}
to
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
agrees with the function
\widetilde{s}
given in Lemma
2.5
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{sgn}(\mathfrak{e})>0
, the restriction of
\widetilde{s}
to
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
is obtained from the function in Lemma
2.5
by twisting by the character
t\mapsto t
of
\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
.

Lemma 2.13
.

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {twocoz0}}}{{=}}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 2.14
.

If
\psi=\psi_{0}
, then
\widetilde{s}(g)=1
, for
g\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
,
g=\omega
, and
g=h(a)
.

Proof.

1) If
g\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
, then
m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)=1=\overline{s}(g)
.

2) If
g=\omega
, then
m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)=e^{\tfrac{-i\pi}{4}}
and
\overline{s}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}
.

3) If
g=h(-1)
, then
m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}
and
\overline{s}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}
.

4) If
g=h(a)
with
\operatorname{sgn}(a)<0
, then
m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}
and
\overline{s}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

If
\psi=\psi_{0}
, then
\widetilde{s}(g)\widetilde{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=1
, for any
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

1) If
g\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
, then
g^{h_{-1}}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
. So
\widetilde{s}(g)=\widetilde{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=1
.

2) If
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
b&a\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then
g^{h_{-1}}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Write
u=a+bi=e^{it}
with
-\pi\leq t<\pi
. Then
\overline{u}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}e^{-it},&-\pi<t<\pi,\\
-1,&t=-\pi.\end{array}\right.

a) If
-\pi<t<\pi
, then
\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=e^{it/2}e^{-it/2}=1
.
m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi[-\operatorname{sgn}(b)]}{4}}
,
m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g^{h_{-1}})=^{\tfrac{i\pi[\operatorname{sgn}(b)]}{4}}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{s}(g)\widetilde{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=1
.

b) If
t=-\pi
, then
\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=e^{-i\pi/2}e^{-i\pi/2}=-1
.
m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)=e^{\tfrac{i\pi}{2}}=m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g^{h_{-1}})
. Therefore,
\widetilde{s}(g)\widetilde{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=-1(i^{2})=1
.

3) If
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we write
g=pk
, for
p\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
and
k\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
, then
p^{h_{-1}}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
and
k^{h_{-1}}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. So
\widetilde{s}(g)=\widetilde{s}(p)\widetilde{s}(k)
and
\widetilde{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=\widetilde{s}(p^{h_{-1}})\widetilde{s}(k^{h_{-1}})
. Hence the result holds.
∎

As a consequence, we have the following group isomorphisms:

\overline{\operatorname{SL}}^{\mu_{8}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R});[g,\epsilon]\longmapsto[g,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)\epsilon],

(2.10)

\overline{\operatorname{SL}}^{\mu_{8}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\prime}}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Im}(\iota^{\prime})(\subseteq\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}));[g,\epsilon]\longmapsto[g,\overline{s}(g)^{-1}\epsilon],

(2.11)

\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\prime}}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Im}(\iota^{\prime})(\subseteq\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}));[g,\epsilon]\longmapsto[g,\widetilde{s}(g)^{-1}\epsilon].

(2.12)

2.4.
The discrete group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

Let

g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Recall the definitions of Dedekind sums from
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
:

•

((x))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x-[x]-\tfrac{1}{2}&\textrm{ if }x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z},\\
0&\textrm{ if }x\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{array}\right.

•

s(d,c)=\sum_{k\bmod(|c|)}((\tfrac{k}{c}))((\tfrac{kd}{c}))
, for two coprime integers
c,d
.(Dedekind sum)

•

s(-d,c)=-s(d,c)
,
s(d,-c)=s(d,c)
.

In
[
2
]
, Asai gave the following function:

\mu(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tfrac{b}{12d}+\tfrac{1-\operatorname{sgn}(d)}{4}&\textrm{ if }c=0,\\
\tfrac{a+d}{12c}-\operatorname{sgn}(c)\bigg(\tfrac{1}{4}+s(d,|c|)\bigg)&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

Define:

•

\overline{\beta}_{1}(g)=e^{-\pi i\mu(g)}
.

•

\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(g)=\overline{\beta}_{1}(g)m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g).

•

\widehat{\beta}_{1}(g)=\overline{\beta}_{1}(g)\overline{s}(g)^{-1}.

Let
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 2.16
.

(1)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

(2)

\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(g_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(g_{2})^{-1}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

(3)

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widehat{\beta}_{1}(g_{1})^{-1}\widehat{\beta}_{1}(g_{2})^{-1}\widehat{\beta}_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

Proof.

1) See
[
2
, Sections 2-3]
.

2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=[\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})][\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})]^{-1}[\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})]^{-1}.

3)

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=[\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1})]^{-1}\cdot[\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{2})]^{-1}\cdot[\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}\overline{\beta}_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})].

∎

2.5.
The discrete group
\Gamma_{\theta}

Let
c,d
be two coprime integers with
cd\neq 0
. Following
[
18
, p.162]
, let us define the symplectic Gauss sum:

G(c,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}|d|^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{|d|-1}e^{-\tfrac{\pi icn^{2}}{d}}.

If
c=0
,
d=\pm 1
, we also define

G(c,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}|d|^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{|d|-1}e^{-\tfrac{\pi icn^{2}}{d}}=1.

Lemma 2.17
.

If
cd\neq 0
is even,
G(d,c)G(c,d)=e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(cd)}
.

Proof.

See
[
18
, p.170, Prop.]
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

For two non-zero coprime integers
c,d
, with
2\mid cd
, we have:

G(d,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{|d|})e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(cd)}&\textrm{ if }c=2c^{\prime},|d|\equiv 1(\bmod 4),\\
i\operatorname{sgn}(d)(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{|d|})e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(cd)}&\textrm{ if }c=2c^{\prime},|d|\equiv 3(\bmod 4),\\
(\tfrac{d^{\prime}}{|c|})&\textrm{ if }d=2d^{\prime},|c|\equiv 1(\bmod 4),\\
-i\operatorname{sgn}(c)(\tfrac{d^{\prime}}{|c|})&\textrm{ if }d=2d^{\prime},|c|\equiv 3(\bmod 4).\end{array}\right.

Proof.

By the above lemma,
G(c,d)G(d,c)=e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{4}\operatorname{sgn}(cd)}
. Moreover, by
[
4
, Thm.1.5.2]
, for
d>0
,
2\mid c
and
c=2c^{\prime}
, we have:

G(c,d)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{d})&\textrm{ if }d\equiv 1(\bmod 4),\\
-(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{d})i&\textrm{ if }d\equiv 3(\bmod 4).\end{array}\right.

If
d<0
, and
c=2c^{\prime}
, then

G(c,d)=\overline{G(c,-d)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{|d|})&\textrm{ if }|d|\equiv 1(\bmod 4),\\
(\tfrac{c^{\prime}}{|d|})i&\textrm{ if }|d|\equiv 3(\bmod 4).\end{array}\right.

Then the result follows.
∎

Following
[
18
, p.155]
), for
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{\theta}
, let us define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}G(d,c)&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0,\\
1&\textrm{ if }c=0.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\overline{\beta}(g)=\widetilde{\beta}(g)m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)^{-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}G(d,c)e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{4}}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0,\\
e^{-\tfrac{\pi i[1-\operatorname{sgn}(d)]}{4}}&\textrm{ if }c=0.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}(g)=\widetilde{\beta}(g)m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)^{-1}\overline{s}(g)^{-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}G(d,c)e^{\tfrac{\pi i\operatorname{sgn}(c)}{4}}\Big[\tfrac{\sqrt{ci+d}}{\sqrt[4]{c^{2}+d^{2}}}\Big]^{-1}&\textrm{ if }c\neq 0,\\
1&\textrm{ if }c=0,\textrm{ or }d=0.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{\theta}
.

(1)

If
\psi=\psi_{0}
,
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{\beta}(g_{2})^{-1}\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

(2)

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{\beta}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{\beta}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{\beta}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

(3)

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widehat{\beta}(g_{1})^{-1}\widehat{\beta}(g_{2})^{-1}\widehat{\beta}(g_{1}g_{2})
.

Proof.

1) See
[
18
, pp.149–150]
.

2)

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{2})\widetilde{c}_{{X^{\ast}}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=[\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}][\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1})^{-1}]^{-1}[\widetilde{\beta}(g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{2})^{-1}]^{-1}.

3)

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=[\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}][\widetilde{\beta}(g_{1})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{1})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{1})^{-1}]^{-1}[\widetilde{\beta}(g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{s}(g_{2})^{-1}]^{-1}.

If
c=0
,
d=\pm 1
and
\widetilde{\beta}(g)m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)^{-1}\overline{s}(g)^{-1}=e^{-\tfrac{\pi i[1-\operatorname{sgn}(d)]}{4}}\Big[\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{|d|}}\Big]^{-1}=1
.
∎

Note that when
\psi=\psi_{0}
,
\widetilde{\beta}
differs from
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}
by a character
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}
.
Let
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(g)=\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(g)/\widetilde{\beta}(g)
, for
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_{\theta}
. According to A. Putman’s answer on the question titled ‘Generators for congruence group
\Gamma_{\theta}
’ on MathOverflow,
\Gamma(2)/\{\pm I\}
is a free group with two generators
u(2)=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},u_{-}(2)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
2&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}
is a character of
\Gamma_{\theta}
, which is determined by
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(\omega)
,
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(\pm I)
,
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(u(2))
,
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(u_{-}(2))
.

Lemma 2.20
.

\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(\omega)=\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(\pm I)=1
,
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(u(2))=e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}
,
\chi_{\Gamma_{\theta}}(u_{-}(2))=e^{\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}
.

Proof.

1)
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(\omega)=\overline{\beta}_{1}(g)m_{X^{\ast},\psi_{0}}(g)=e^{-\pi i(-\tfrac{1}{4})}\cdot e^{-\tfrac{i\pi}{4}}=1
,
\widetilde{\beta}(\omega)=1
.

2)
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(u(2))=e^{-\pi i\tfrac{1}{6}}
,
\widetilde{\beta}(u(2))=1
.

3)
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}(u_{-}(2))=e^{-\pi i\tfrac{1}{12}}
,
\widetilde{\beta}(u_{-}(2))=e^{-\pi i\tfrac{1}{4}}
.
∎

2.6.
Automorphic factor I

Define:

(1)

J(g,z)=cz+d
;

(2)

\alpha_{z}(g)=\tfrac{cz+d}{|cz+d|}
;

(2)

J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)=\epsilon(g;z,z_{0})\cdot|J(g,z)|^{1/2}
; (Coming from
[
31
, p.131,
\wasyparagraph
5]
)

(3)

J_{1/2}(g,z)=J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)\cdot\widetilde{s}(g)
;

(4)

J_{3/2}(g,z)=J_{1/2}(g,z)J(g,z)
.

Lemma 2.21
.

J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)^{2}=\alpha_{z_{0}}(g)^{-1}J(g,z)
and
J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{1}g_{2},z)=J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})
.

Proof.

See
[
31
, pp. 131–132]
; we supply the details for completeness.

1)
J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)^{2}=\epsilon(g;z,z_{0})^{2}\cdot|J(g,z)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem.}\ref {epzz'}}}{{=}}e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}(cz+d)-\operatorname{Arg}(cz_{0}+d)\Big]}\cdot|J(g,z)|=[\tfrac{cz_{0}+d}{|cz_{0}+d|}]^{-1}(cz+d)=\alpha_{z_{0}}(g)^{-1}J(g,z)
.

2)Put
g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}
,
z^{\prime}=g_{2}z
,
z^{\prime\prime}=g_{2}z_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\epsilon(g_{1};g_{2}z,g_{2}z_{0})

\displaystyle=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z^{\prime\prime}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}

\displaystyle=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z^{\prime}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z_{0}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z_{0}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((c_{1}z^{\prime\prime}+d_{1})^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}

\displaystyle=\epsilon(g_{1};g_{2}z,z_{0})\epsilon(g_{1};z_{0},g_{2}z_{0}).

Hence

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{1}g_{2},z)=

\displaystyle\epsilon(g_{1}g_{2};z,z_{0})\cdot|J(g_{1}g_{2},z)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma(g_{1}g_{2}z,g_{1}g_{2}z_{0})}{\gamma(z,z_{0})}\cdot|J(g_{1}g_{2},z)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon(g_{1};g_{2}z,g_{2}z_{0})\epsilon(g_{2};z,z_{0})\cdot|J(g_{1}g_{2},z)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon(g_{1};z_{0},g_{2}z_{0})\epsilon(g_{1};g_{2}z,z_{0})\epsilon(g_{2};z,z_{0})\cdot|J(g_{1},g_{2}z)|^{1/2}|J(g_{2},z)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\epsilon(g_{1};g_{2}z,z_{0})|J(g_{1},g_{2}z)|^{1/2}\cdot\epsilon(g_{2};z,z_{0})|J(g_{2},z)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2}).

∎

Remark 2.22
.

J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z_{0})=|\det J(g,z_{0})|^{1/2}=1
and
J_{1/2}(g,z_{0})=\widetilde{s}(g)
, for
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
.

For
z\in\mathbb{H}
, let
p_{z}
be the corresponding element in
P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.23
.

J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)=\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g,p_{z})^{-1}\cdot|J(g,z)|^{1/2}
.

Proof.

It follows from that
\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g,p_{z})=\epsilon(g;z_{0},z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {epzz'}}}{{=}}\epsilon(g;z,z_{0})^{-1}
.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

J_{1/2}(g_{1}g_{2},z)=J_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})
.

Proof.

Left side

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{s}(g_{2})^{-1}J_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {eightcoz0}}}{{=}}\text{Right side}.

∎

Lemma 2.25
.

[J_{1/2}(g,z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}]^{2}=cz+d
.

Proof.

\text{Left side}=[J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)\widetilde{s}(g)m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}]^{2}=[J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)\overline{s}(g)]^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Eq.}\ref {eqalpha}}}{{=}}\alpha_{z_{0}}(g)^{-1}J(g,z)\cdot\alpha_{z_{0}}(g)=\text{Right side}
.
∎

Definition 2.26
.

\sqrt{cz+d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Def.}}{{=}}J_{1/2}(g,z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 2.27
.

The above definition is compatible with our choice of
\operatorname{Arg}
.

Proof.

J_{1/2}(g,z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}=J^{\prime}_{1/2}(g,z)\overline{s}(g)=e^{i\Big[\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz+d)^{1/2}\Big)-\operatorname{Arg}\Big((cz_{0}+d)^{1/2}\Big)\Big]}|J(g,z)|^{1/2}\Big(\tfrac{J(g,z_{0})}{|J(g,z_{0})|}\Big)^{1/2}=(cz+d)^{1/2}
.
∎

Let
g_{3}=g_{1}g_{2}
and
g_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{pmatrix}
.

Lemma 2.28
.

\sqrt{c_{3}z+d_{3}}=\sqrt{c_{1}(g_{2}z)+d_{1}}\cdot\sqrt{c_{2}z+d_{2}}\cdot\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})
.

Proof.

Left side

\displaystyle=J_{1/2}(g_{1}g_{2},z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lem.}\ref {J121J1}}}{{=}}J_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J_{1/2}(g_{2},z)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=J_{1/2}(g_{1},g_{2}(z))J_{1/2}(g_{2},z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2})^{-1}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\text{Right side}.

∎

For
\overline{g}=(g,\epsilon)\in\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
or
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}^{\mu_{8}}(\mathbb{R})
, define:

\displaystyle J_{1/2}(\overline{g},z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\epsilon^{-1}J_{1/2}(g,z)m_{X^{\ast}}(g)^{-1}=\epsilon^{-1}\sqrt{cz+d},

\displaystyle J_{3/2}(\overline{g},z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq J_{1/2}(\overline{g},z)\,J(g,z)=\epsilon^{-1}\sqrt{cz+d}(cz+d).

Let
\kappa=1
or
3
.

Corollary 2.29
.

For every
\overline{g}_{1},\overline{g}_{2}\in\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
J_{\kappa/2}(\overline{g}_{1}\overline{g}_{2},z)=J_{\kappa/2}(\overline{g}_{1},g_{2}z)\,J_{\kappa/2}(\overline{g}_{2},z)
.

Corollary 2.30
.

J_{\kappa/2}(-,z_{0}):\overline{\operatorname{SO}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C});\overline{g}\longmapsto J_{\kappa/2}(\overline{g},z_{0})
is a group homomorphism.

3.
The relevant covering groups:
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
case

Following Barthel
[
3
]
, who extended the 2-cocycle
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
from
\operatorname{Sp}
to
\operatorname{GSp}
over a
p
-adic field, we carry out the analogous construction over
\mathbb{R}
. In this section we extend the 2-cocycles
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
,
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
, and
\widehat{c}_{z}
from
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to the full group
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.1.
\mu_{8}
-covering

Let
\alpha
now be a continuous automorphism of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By Moore’s cohomology theory, there exists a unique automorphism
\alpha^{\ast}
of
\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that the following diagram

\begin{CD}1@>{}>{}>\mu_{8}@>{}>{}>\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})@>{}>{}>\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})@>{}>{}>1\\
\Big\|@V{}V{\alpha^{\ast}}V@V{}V{\alpha}V\\
1@>{}>{}>\mu_{8}@>{}>{}>\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})@>{}>{}>\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})@>{}>{}>1\end{CD}

is commutative. Moreover, there exists a unique function
\nu(\alpha,-):\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mu_{8}
, such that the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu(\alpha,g_{1})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{2})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2}).

(3.1)

The map
\alpha^{\ast}
is given as:

\alpha^{\ast}:\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R});[g,\epsilon]\longmapsto[g^{\alpha},\nu(\alpha,g)\epsilon].

Lemma 3.1
.

For two automorphisms
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
,
\nu(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},g)=\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\nu(\alpha_{2},g^{\alpha_{1}})
.

Proof.

Because
(g,\epsilon)^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=(g^{\alpha_{1}},\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\epsilon)^{\alpha_{2}}=(g^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}},\nu(\alpha_{1},g)\nu(\alpha_{2},g^{\alpha_{1}})\epsilon)
.
∎

Example 3.2
.

If
\alpha=h\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
h
acts on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by conjugation, i.e.,
g^{h}=h^{-1}gh
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\nu(\alpha,g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},gh)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h)
.

Proof.

By the properties of cocycles, we obtain:

\tfrac{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{h},g_{2}^{h})}{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})}=[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{1}g_{2}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}g_{2},h)][\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{1}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},h)]^{-1}[\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g_{2}h)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{2},h)]^{-1}.

(3.2)

So
\nu(\alpha,g)=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},gh)\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h)
by the uniqueness.
∎

Example 3.3

(Barthel)
.

If
\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then

\displaystyle\nu(\alpha,g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(y,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&c=0,\\
(c,y)_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}&c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the
p
-adic case in
[
3
, p.212, Prop.1.2.A]
.
∎

From now on, if
\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}
, we will write
\nu(y,g)
for
\nu(\alpha,g)
, and
g^{y}
for
g^{\alpha}
.
There exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}^{\times}\longrightarrow 1.

Let us choose a section map

\displaystyle s:\mathbb{R}^{\times}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R});y\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}.

(3.5)

Then
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By lifting the action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
from
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain a group
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
; let us denote it by
\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists an exact sequence:

1\longrightarrow\mu_{8}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow 1.

Let
\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}
denote the
2
-cocycle associated to this exact sequence from
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}
. For
(y_{1},g_{1},\epsilon_{1}),(y_{2},g_{2},\epsilon_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\begin{split}(y_{1},g_{1},\epsilon_{1})\cdot(y_{2},g_{2},\epsilon_{2})&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1},\epsilon_{1}]^{y_{2}}[g_{2},\epsilon_{2}])\\
&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1}^{y_{2}},\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\epsilon_{1}][g_{2},\epsilon_{2}])\\
&=(y_{1}y_{2},[g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2},\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}]).\end{split}

(3.6)

Therefore, we obtain:

Lemma 3.4
.

\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2}),

(3.7)

for
[y_{i},g_{i}]\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.2.
\mu_{2}
-covering

Instead of the
8
-degree cover, let us consider the
2
-degree covering case. For an automorphism
\alpha
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, let us define
\nu_{2}:\alpha\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mu_{2}
such that

(g,\epsilon)^{\alpha}=(g^{\alpha},\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)\epsilon),\qquad\qquad(g,\epsilon)\in\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly,
\nu_{2}
determines an automorphism of
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
iff the following equality holds:

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})\nu_{2}(\alpha,g_{1})\nu_{2}(\alpha,g_{2})\nu_{2}(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

(3.8)

Lemma 3.5
.

For two automorphisms
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
,
\nu_{2}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},g)=\nu_{2}(\alpha_{1},g)\nu_{2}(\alpha_{2},g^{\alpha_{1}})
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to that of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)}{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g^{\alpha})}
.

Proof.

\begin{split}&\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})^{-1}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. \ref {relacto}}}}{{=}}\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref {chap3equ})}}{{=}}\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})\nu(\alpha,g_{1})\nu(\alpha,g_{2})\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. \ref {relacto}}}}{{=}}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}^{\alpha}g_{2}^{\alpha})m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}^{\alpha})^{-1}m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2}^{\alpha})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{1})\nu(\alpha,g_{2})\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.\end{split}

Hence:

\tfrac{\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})}{\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})}=[\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}g_{2})}{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}^{\alpha}g_{2}^{\alpha})}]^{-1}\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})}{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}^{\alpha})}\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})}{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2}^{\alpha})}\nu(\alpha,g_{1})\nu(\alpha,g_{2})\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

According to the above (
3.8
), we have
\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g)}{m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g^{\alpha})}
.
∎

Example 3.7
.

If
\alpha=h\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
h
acts on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by conjugation, i.e.,
g^{h}=h^{-1}gh
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\nu_{2}(\alpha,g)=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},gh)\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,h)=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1},g)\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(h^{-1}g,h)
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to that of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Example 3.8
.

Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}(d,-bc)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }d\neq 0,c\neq 0,b\neq 0,\\
(d,ac)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }d\neq 0,c\neq 0,b=0,\\
(d,b)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }d\neq 0,c=0,b\neq 0,\\
1&\textrm{ if }d\neq 0,c=0,b=0,\\
(-c,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}&\textrm{ if }d=0,c\neq 0,b\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

1)
h=\omega
, then
g\omega=\begin{pmatrix}b&-a\\
d&-c\end{pmatrix}
and
\omega^{-1}g\omega=\begin{pmatrix}d&-c\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}
. Then:

\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega),

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(x(g),x(\omega))_{\mathbb{R}}(-x(g)x(\omega),x(g\omega))_{\mathbb{R}},

\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(x(\omega^{-1}),x(g\omega))_{\mathbb{R}}(-x(\omega^{-1})x(g\omega),x(\omega^{-1}g\omega))_{\mathbb{R}}.

(1)

If
d\neq 0
,
c\neq 0
and
b\neq 0
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(c,1)_{\mathbb{R}}(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}=(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}=(d,-bc)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(2)

If
d\neq 0
,
c\neq 0
and
b=0
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(c,1)_{\mathbb{R}}(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}=(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,a)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=(-c,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,a)_{\mathbb{R}}=(d,ac)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(3)

If
d\neq 0
,
c=0
and
b\neq 0
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(d,1)_{\mathbb{R}}(-d,d)_{\mathbb{R}}=1
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}=(d,b)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(4)

If
d\neq 0
,
c=0
and
b=0
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(d,1)_{\mathbb{R}}(-d,d)_{\mathbb{R}}=1
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,a)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=(-1,d)_{\mathbb{R}}(d,a)_{\mathbb{R}}=(d,-a)_{\mathbb{R}}=(d,-d^{-1})_{\mathbb{R}}=1
.

(5)

If
d=0
,
c\neq 0
and
b\neq 0
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g,\omega)=(c,1)_{\mathbb{R}}(-c,-c)_{\mathbb{R}}=(-c,-c)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(\omega^{-1},g\omega)=(-1,-c)_{\mathbb{R}}(-c,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\nu_{2}(\omega,g)=(-c,-c)_{\mathbb{R}}(-1,-c)_{\mathbb{R}}(-c,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}=(-c,-b)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

∎

Lemma 3.9
.

For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, we have:

\nu_{2}(y,g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(y,a)_{\mathbb{R}}&c=0,\\
1&c\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

(3.9)

Proof.

1) If
c=0
,
m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g)=m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g^{y})
, so
\nu_{2}(y,g)=\nu(y,g)=(y,a)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

2) If
c\neq 0
, then:

m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g)=\gamma(c,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}\gamma(\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}=\gamma({\psi}^{\tfrac{1}{2}c})^{-1}
,

m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g^{y})=\gamma({\psi}^{\tfrac{1}{2}cy^{-1}})^{-1}
,

\tfrac{m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g)}{m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g^{y})}=\gamma(y^{-1},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})(c,y)_{\mathbb{R}}
,

\nu_{2}(y,g)=\nu(y,g)\tfrac{m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g)}{m_{{X^{\ast},\psi}}(g^{y})}=(c,y)_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(y,\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})^{-1}\gamma(y^{-1},\psi^{\tfrac{1}{2}})(c,y)_{\mathbb{R}}=1
.

∎

Lifting the natural action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to the two–fold cover
\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
produces the semidirect product

\overline{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\overline{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),

which fits into the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\overline{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow 1.

We denote the corresponding
2
-cocycle on
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by
\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}
. Explicitly,

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=\nu_{2}(y_{2},g_{1})\;\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2}),\qquad[y_{i},g_{i}]\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Inserting the identity from Lemma
3.6
yields

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}])=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2})^{-1}\,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{1})\,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g_{2})\,\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}]).

(3.10)

Abusing notation, we set

m_{X^{\ast},\psi}[y,g]=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(g),\qquad[y,g]\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Then, for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})=m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1}\,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(h_{1})\,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(h_{2})\,\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2}).

(3.11)

3.3.
Link
\widehat{c}_{z}
to
\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}
and
\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}

Assume
z=p_{z}(i)
.

Lemma 3.10
.

(1)

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}
.

(2)

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}
.

Proof.

1)

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {ztoz0}}}{{=}}\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{p_{z}},g_{2}^{p_{z}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {twocoz0}}}{{=}}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{p_{z}},g_{2}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Eq. }\ref {chap3crao2}}}{{=}}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu_{2}(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}.

2)

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {ztoz0}}}{{=}}\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{p_{z}},g_{2}^{p_{z}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem. }\ref {eightcoz0}}}{{=}}\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{p_{z}},g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Eq. }\ref {chap3equ}}}{{=}}\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}.

∎

3.4.
One Lemma

Lemma 3.11
.

\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{h_{-1}},g_{2}^{h_{-1}})=1
, for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1},g_{2})\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{h_{-1}},g_{2}^{h_{-1}})

\displaystyle=[\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g^{h_{-1}}_{1},g^{h_{-1}}_{2})][\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}}_{1})][\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}}_{2})][\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{h_{-1}}g_{2}^{h_{-1}})]^{-1}

\displaystyle=[\nu_{2}(-1,g_{1})\nu_{2}(-1,g_{2})\nu_{2}(-1,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}][\overline{s}(g_{1})\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}}_{1})][\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}}_{2})][\overline{s}(g_{1}g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{h_{-1}}g_{2}^{h_{-1}})]^{-1}.

Let
g=pk_{g}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
p\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R})
and
k_{g}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
. Then

p^{h_{-1}}\in P_{>0}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad k_{g}^{h_{-1}}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C}),\qquad g^{h_{-1}}=p^{h_{-1}}k_{g}^{h_{-1}},

and

\nu_{2}(-1,g)=\nu_{2}(-1,k_{g}),\qquad\overline{s}(g)=\overline{s}(k_{g}).

For any
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
, one has

\nu_{2}(-1,g)=\overline{s}(g)\overline{s}(g^{h_{-1}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-1&\textrm{ if }g=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\\
1&\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Consequently, we may (and do) assume
g_{i}\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbb{C})
and the claimed identity follows.
∎

3.5.
\mathbb{T}
-covering

Rather than working with the two–fold cover itself, we now focus on the cocycle
\widehat{c}_{z}
.
For every automorphism
\alpha
of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
we introduce a map

\nu_{z}\colon\alpha\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}

such that

(g,\epsilon)^{\alpha}=\bigl(g^{\alpha},\nu_{z}(\alpha,g)\,\epsilon\bigr),\qquad(g,\epsilon)\in\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R}).

This
\nu_{z}
extends to an automorphism of
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R})
precisely when

\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})\,\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{1})\,\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{2})\,\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}.

(3.12)

Let us determine the explicit form of
\nu_{z}(\alpha,g)
.

Lemma 3.12
.

\nu_{z}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{\nu(p_{z},g^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g^{p_{z}})}{\nu(p_{z},g)\widetilde{s}(g^{{\alpha}p_{z}})}
, for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\tfrac{\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})}{\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1},g_{2})}=\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{1})^{-1}\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{2})^{-1}\nu_{z}(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem.}\ref {beznu2nu}}}{{=}}\tfrac{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{\alpha},g_{2}^{\alpha})\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2}^{\alpha})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha}g_{2}^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}}g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})^{-1}}{\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1},g_{2})\nu(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2}^{\alpha})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha}g_{2}^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}}g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})^{-1}}{\nu(p_{z},g_{1})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{2})^{-1}\nu(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})^{-1}}\nu(\alpha,g_{1})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{2})^{-1}\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})

\displaystyle=\Big[\tfrac{\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}})\nu(\alpha,g_{1})}{\nu(p_{z},g_{1})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})}\Big]^{-1}\Big[\tfrac{\nu(p_{z},g_{2}^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{p_{z}})\nu(\alpha,g_{2})}{\nu(p_{z},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})}\Big]^{-1}\Big[\tfrac{\nu(p_{z},g_{1}^{\alpha}g_{2}^{\alpha})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{p_{z}}g_{2}^{p_{z}})\nu(\alpha,g_{1}g_{2})}{\nu(p_{z},g_{1}g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{{\alpha}p_{z}}g_{2}^{{\alpha}p_{z}})}\Big].

∎

Example 3.13
.

\nu_{z_{0}}(\alpha,g)=\nu(\alpha,g)\tfrac{\widetilde{s}(g)}{\widetilde{s}(g^{{\alpha}})}
.

Example 3.14
.

If
\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}
, then

\nu_{z_{0}}(y,g)=\nu(y,g)\tfrac{\widetilde{s}(g)}{\widetilde{s}(g^{y})}=\nu_{2}(y,g)\tfrac{\overline{s}(g)}{\overline{s}(g^{y})}
.

Lifting the natural action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R})
yields the semidirect product

\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R})

fitting into the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}^{z}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow 1.

We retain the symbol
\widehat{C}_{z}
for the corresponding
2
-cocycle on
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
; explicitly,

\widehat{C}_{z}\bigl([y_{1},g_{1}],[y_{2},g_{2}]\bigr)=\nu_{z}(y_{2},g_{1})\,\widehat{c}_{z}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2}),\qquad[y_{i},g_{i}]\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(3.13)

Remark 3.15
.

For simplicity, when
z=z_{0}
we write

\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}^{z_{0}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=\widehat{\operatorname{SL}}^{z_{0}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

For
h=[y,g]\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, let us also write:

\widetilde{s}(h)=\widetilde{s}(g),\qquad\overline{s}(h)=\overline{s}(g).

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
h_{i}=[y_{i},g_{i}]\in F^{\times}\ltimes\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

(1)

\widehat{C}_{z_{0}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1})\overline{s}(h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1}
.

(2)

\widehat{C}_{z_{0}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\widetilde{s}(h_{1})\widetilde{s}(h_{2})\widetilde{s}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1}
.

Proof.

1)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{z_{0}}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref {betaz})}}{{=}}\nu_{z_{0}}(y_{2},g_{1})\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Ex.}\ref {exz0z}}}{{=}}\nu_{2}(y_{2},g)\tfrac{\overline{s}(g)}{\overline{s}(g^{y_{2}})}\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem.}\ref {beznu2nu}(1)}}{{=}}\nu_{2}(y_{2},g)\tfrac{\overline{s}(g)}{\overline{s}(g^{y_{2}})}\overline{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})\overline{s}(g_{2})\overline{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1})\overline{s}(h_{2})\overline{s}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1}.

2)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{z_{0}}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref {betaz})}}{{=}}\nu_{z_{0}}(y_{2},g_{1})\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Ex.}\ref {exz0z}}}{{=}}\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\tfrac{\widetilde{s}(g_{1})}{\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})}\widehat{c}_{z_{0}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{ Lem.}\ref {beznu2nu}(2)}}{{=}}\nu(y_{2},g_{1})\tfrac{\widetilde{s}(g_{1})}{\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})}\widetilde{c}_{X^{\ast}}(g_{1}^{y_{2}},g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}})\widetilde{s}(g_{2})\widetilde{s}(g_{1}^{y_{2}}g_{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{X^{\ast}}(h_{1},h_{2})\widetilde{s}(h_{1})\widetilde{s}(h_{2})\widetilde{s}(h_{1}h_{2})^{-1}.

∎

As a consequence, we have the following group isomorphisms:

\overline{\operatorname{GL}}^{\mu_{8}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\widetilde{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R});[h,\epsilon]\longmapsto[h,m_{X^{\ast},\psi}(h)\epsilon],

(3.14)

\overline{\operatorname{GL}}^{\mu_{8}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\prime}}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Im}(\iota^{\prime})(\subseteq\widehat{\operatorname{GL}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}));[h,\epsilon]\longmapsto[h,\overline{s}(h)^{-1}\epsilon],

(3.15)
